DIFFERENCES IN LAW 
~ REVEALED 


Conflict Between Chinese 


And US. 


Codes. 


Shanghai, Inne 17, 


A point of difference between Chinese and American law 
occupied most of the morning in the United ‘States Court for 


China yesterday in the case, of Chen Wong-shih against 


Mr. 


Charles H. Raven, Jr. who is the defendant on a charge of kill- | 
ing a Chinese In an automobile aceident. Mr, -JB. Davies, at- 
torney for Mr. Raven, filed a demurrer, against the proceedings, 


Mr, Davies contended that un- 
less the plaintlf? wag actually the 
administrator of the deceased's 
‘estate, she was not entitled to spe. 

“| His. point was that. if the wife of; 
the deceased could ‘sue’ successful- 


decqased could also sue. 
a number of cases in the Court of 
Appeals in the District of Colum- 
bia, where it was unheld that only 
the administrator of the deceased 
could sue, thus giving the defen- 
dant the protection of being. sued 
‘only once should the sujt be suc- 
‘ cessful. 4 
‘In the, present~case.,, however; he 
‘went on, the wife of the deceased, 
{Was not-the duly appointed’ ad- 
{ministrator of the estate under 
: American jaw, and thus could not 
i sue"his client’ until she had made 
herself ‘so.: 


. DIFFERENCE ELUCIDATED 


In reply Mr. Myron Weiner, at- 
torney for the deceased; stated 
that in Chinese law the nearest 
relative, in his case the wife, auto- 
matically became the administra+ 
tor of the estate without the ap- 
pointment of the Court.” He, 
brought Chen Wong-shih to the 
stand to explain his point. 

Chen Wong-shihy a Chinese at- 
torey, explained when ‘and when 
not \t was necessary for the ad- 
ministrator to “be appointed. If 
the living relatives of a deceased 
man were not’ known or if there 
was no estate then the Court ap-! 
pointed an administrator, although 
in the latter case, where the dés 
ceased left no estate it was usually , 
unnecessary for the Cow 


then it wos: ‘wnnecessary for the 
Court to act, the relative auto- 
matically becomitig the adminis- 


| trator. 


This was in direct contradistinc~ 
‘tion. to American law which re- 


| ly, then all of the relatives of the | quires. that’ the administratar or 
He cited | the deceased be appointed by the 
| Court and that the administrator 


alone might sue or be sued, 

Mr. Davies made the point in 
reply’ that all who came into the 
US,.Court must comply with Ame- 
riean law, to,.which Mr. _ Weiner: 
stated that the Chinese: Court 
would Rot nullity its own law duly 
to appoint an administrator. 
Judge Helmick asked several ques- 
tians.on this point and consulted 
the Chinesd Civil Code. ° 


DEMURRER OVERRULED 


In his judgment the judge said 
that he would like to write ‘an 
opinion on the point in question 
as he found it very interesting, 
but that he was sorely pressed for 
time. In his opinion, he sald, he, 
found that the wife of the de- 
ceased, being the nearest living re- 
lative, met the requirements of the 
Chinese Code'as for her capacity 
to be the administrator of the de- 
ceased's estate. Although, he went 


on, it was difficult to harmonize] 


the laws of two different countries, 
he overruled the demurrer, and the 
wife of the deceased was entitled 


‘to sue in the U.S. Court. 


"tf, however,” Judge: Helmick 
said, “I find that I have been mis- 
taken in this decision, then I will 
take steps to correct it.” He stated 


“to take! in conclusion that he did not con- 
action. But in the case of the; 


sider the demurrer well taker, and 


nearest living relative of the de-; that the plaintiff had a right to

London, June 1i.. 


\ [As the Emperor of Abyss'nia was travelling incognito there 
was no official ceremony when he landed at Southampton yes- |. 
terday from the Orient liner Orford, but he was cheered by a 


Fleet Street. 
Thousands of people who. would 
have liked to welcome 
peror of Abyssifia on hts arrivai 


Jn. London to-day were, of course. 


at work when the boat-train that 
brought him from Southampton 
came into Waterloo Station just 
before five o'clock, but two hours 
earlier the police were moving 
back the crowds who had gathered 
in the statlon and who kept stead- 
fastly to their front rows,’ while 
the pressure behind, them steadily 
increased till the crowd was-ten 
deep. It became,so large that te- 
inforcements of police were caled 
up .to deal with it, a 
Bright-red banners were upheld 
the Fritnds «of Abyssinia 
organisation, and people were 
wearing armiets or enamelled 
badges in the Abyssinian colours 
or waving small Abyssinian flags. 
Many coloured people were :n the 
crowd, Including a stalwart man 
who said he came from Abyssinia’s 
nearest neighbour, Kenya, and 
who was tobed in blue silk. 
“Prince Monolulu.” the racing 
Uipster, appeared in his most gor- 
geous dress. and loftiest coloured 
plumes with a Union Jack and 
an Ethiopian flag in elther hand. 
Meantime the-crowds were mas- 
sing along the station. approach 
and. gathering twenty deep on 
either side of York. Road. Only 
people with permits were allowed 
to stand behind the barvier, part 
way down ‘No. il platform, at 
which the train was to arrive. 
They had a good view of- those 
who came to welcome the Emperor. 
‘a group including Mr. O. C. Har- 
vey, private secretary to Mr. 
Anthony Eden; Lord and Lady 
Alen “of Hurtwood, Sir Norman 
Angell, Mr. Vyvyan Adams, MP.. 
Miss Eleanor Rathbone, M.P.,. Sir 
Walter and ‘Lady ‘Layton. Profes- 
sor Stanley Jevons (honorary secre- 
of the Abyssinian Assdcla- 
tion), and Miss Margery Per- 
ham, the student of the admiuis- 
tration of mandates, Several wo- 
men were walting to present 
bouquets to the Emperor and the 
‘PrinceSses. ee ” 
AN INFORMAL WELCUME 
. There was. no red carpet on this

o(aforeign monarch so stirred the

sonally, — =

our and formality four years ago 
—looking out eagerly from the

plexioned, bearded, lustrous-eyed. 
who. might have posed for the 
,picture of. an ‘apostle, rather than 
a, ruler, the head of a Church 
rather than the head ‘of an army.

entered, his coach one expected to

cast of p.ayers in the flmization 
of Clarence E, Muiford’s new story 
for Paramount’d “The, Eagle's 
Brood” that opens to-day at the 
Queen's Theatre. Portraying ‘Hop- 
along Cassidy” Mulford’s famous 
western fiction character, Boyd 
impersonates one of the ald west's 


fearless gun-fghters. 
. In, “The Eagle's Brood’ “Hop- 
“along” has become a man of the 
law's peace officer in the great 
| Southwest who can use his] head 
as well as his,hands. Jimmy 
| eiugon, new western star, {3 seen 
‘lin the ro‘e of “Johnny Nelson,” 
youthful “pay” of Boppy, who 
{dolizs him’ and of whom 
“Boppy” is continually. trying to} 
keep out of. scrapes. Together! 
they ferret out a band of “Bad- 
"men" who have held the town of 
Hell Centre in a reign of terror 
and through strategy they succeed 
“'n wipping them out and restoring 
Jaw and" order to the community,

BEST ALL-ROUND GUN 
As to the vexed question of 


for the different categories ' of 
fighting ships, I venture to think 
that an intelligent answer would 
be “the smallest gun which {s per- 
}fectly adequate to doing its. job.” 
The size of the gun more than any 
other single factor has brought 
‘about the immense growth in the 
size Of the ship. In 1900 we had’ 
ee 12in, gun welghing. 50 tons 
unmounted and the 15.000-tons 
, battleship to carry. it. A .quarter 
|of a century later we have the 
16in. gun. weighing 1035 tons un- 
mounted and a 34,000-tons: battle- 
ship to carry It. There has béen 


jsuch an improvement in weapons . | 


‘and ballistics that an @in, gun 
ceulser of to-day could knock-out 
fe '12in. gun battleship of 1900, 
w.thout being scratched. We want 
weapons with which to’ fight 
“any enemy,” but surely !t is pos- - 
sfble to set very practical Umita- ‘ 
| tons to the cult of: size for the 
| mere sake of size. There cames' 
po'nt beyond which size. or rather . 
what size implies in fighting pow- 
er, lg mere extravagance of ‘wasted 
; energy. The British taxpayer has 
ijust been unpleasdhtly, awakened 
{to the fact that we “must do 
things as cheaply as ds possible.” 
Let us be satisfied with ships 
‘which can hit smashing blows up. 
to the full range of visibility from 
thelr observation stations. If” 
other nations Uke to build ‘ships 
| Which can deliver smashing -blows 
twice'as far as the'r peop’e can 
zee, Jet’ them wast? money and’: 
‘ammunition by alll means.

ROYAL: NAVY 
THE ENTRY OF 


“ 
‘(egusaae ® Mail Service) * 
. London, June 10. 
The First Lord of the Admiralty, 
in his letter published in “The 
Times” on May 30 in regard to the 
improved prospects of a career for 


boys who join now as cadets, made | 


no reference ta the question of the 
ages of entry. Patents or guar-’ 
dians .who contemplate putting 
boys into “the Navy’ should distin-" 
gulsh clearly between “entry into 
Dartmouth ‘College, which {s at 
about 134 years'of age, and that by 
the’ special entry examination, 
which ts at about 17% years. 

For the Dartmouth entry, ap- 
plication should not be made until 
the candidate has reached 12) 
years of age. There are three 
entries a year, In January, May. 
and September, and candidates 
must be more than 13 years and. 
four months but not more than 13 
years and eight months of age on 
the preceding December 1, April 1, 
and At 1 respectively. The 
applications must be received at 
the Admiralty as follows:—For the 
January entry. before October 16; 
tar the May entry, before January 
16; and ‘for the September entry.. 
before -May 16. A committee in- 
terviews each applicant separate- 
ly, and ‘there ts also a qualifying 


r examination, 


To be eligible to sit for the civil 
Service examination for a special 
entry cadetship, 2 candidate must 
have attalned the age of 17 and 
must not have attained: the age of 
18 on the"following dates:—June 
examination, July 1 following; 
November examination, January 
1 following. The examinations 
usually begin on the fourth Tues- 
day in June and the third Tuesday 
in November. Successful can- 
didates fotn ‘a training cruiser for 
a year’s service, during which they, 
receive pay at the rate of 1s. 2 day. 
No fees. are payable in respect of 
special entry cadets, but parents 
or guardians are required to make 
on their behalf a private allowance 
at the rate of £36 per annuin dur- 
ing thelr period as cadets. This is. 
for books and similar expenses, 
and is, in no way~ a aera 
allowance to the cadet himself. 

CADETS’ AGE OF ENTRY 

In amplification of the ‘notes on 
the entry of cadets’ published, it 
should be pointed out that the up- 
Per age limit for the special entry 
has recently been raised. It is now 
183 years instead of 18 years. The 
change takes effect with the 
examination which is being held 


COMMISSIONING OF THE 
ROYAL OAK, “ 
The battleship Royal Oak is to 
commission at Devonport for trials 
after large repairs. She has been 
in dockyard hands since "June, 
1934,, and the various improve- 
ments and renewals made in her, 
have cost about £1,000,000. Capt. 
T. B. Drew, OBE., recently attend- 
ing ‘the senior officers’ war course 
and formerly. Deputy Director of 
Personal Services, has been ap- 
pointed in command of the Royal 
Oak. ° 
BATTLE CRUISER SQUADRON 


HLM.S, Repulse, Capt. J. H. God- 


!frey, ‘ls to leave Portsmouth for 


service in the Mediterranean, after 
large repairs at Portsmouth which 
nave .considerably enhanced her 
efficiency.. She will take the place 
of her sister ship the’ Renown, 
which -arrived at Sheerness on 
May 23, and which after giving 
leave will.pay off for large repalrs 


|,at Portsmouth Dockyard, towards 


the cost of which £720,000 is voted 
in the current Navy Estimates. 


The. decision .to transfer the 
Battle Cruiser Squadron’ .to the 
Mediterranean Command was an- 
nouriced by the First Lord on 
March 1, 1935, but it is not yet 
known when the Hood, flagship 
of the Vice-Admiral Commanding, 
will be transferred. For some 
time she has been at, Gibraltar 
with other Home Fleet units: The’ 
Hood. is overdue for recoramission- 
ing, since her present © ship’s 
company joined on August 30, 
1933, and’ it may well be that she 
will, return to England for this 
purpose before long, as the ‘Queen 
Elizabeth has done. -

had wreaths and bouquets of mixed. 
cream flowers. Mr. R. C. Rose, 
Price; The Royal Welch. Pusiliers,” 
was best man. 

‘A reception was held at 13, 
Grosvenor Place, and then the bride , 
and bridegroom leit to spend the

went away in a dress of deep'pink 
crepe, with a black hat and coat, 


and a silver fox fur. | i

A BUSIER BRITAIN - 

Improvement in British trade 
and! ‘employment 
own, There were some who fear- 
ed the peak of. employment had 


been reached last December, and | 


that ‘the seasonal nemployment 
of midwinter would not be follow- 
ed by a pertod better than the cor- 
responding period “of last year. 
But as it’ turned out, there were 
411,000 more. employed in March 
than a year ago and 1,216,000 more 


_ than three years ago. Total un-| 
employment figures, 


though still 
high, had fallen to 1,881,531. 
' There are two reasons for re- 
garding the situation. with some 
satisfaction,’ In the first place it 
might have «been supposed that 
the dlarms and excursions in 


Europe would prove so disturbing, 


to confidence that trade of every 
kind “(except that of the arma- 


ment firms) would suffer a set-: 


‘back: No doubt there have ‘been 
“economic ill consequences follow- 
ing general unrest. But the move- 
ment toward ‘recovery has over- 
come these adverse influences, 
, The second ground for satistac- 
tion Is ‘that improvement "has : oc- 
curred in all the more important 
British trades, except coal-mining. 
It 1s manifest in the building in- 


dustry, in {ron and' steel, engineer- | . 


ing, tHe. automobile trades, in 


“+. wool, cotton, pottery—and, even in 


' shipbullding. 

This has happened without any. 
‘spectacular programme of public 
works on © huge scale. None the 
‘léss, both the Government and 
other big spending ‘agencies have 
been greatiy influenced by recent | 

“demands for’ expenditure designed 
to promote work. There hes been 
‘all over the country much more 
willingness to embark on ‘recan- 
‘struction’ schemes, and these 
cumulatively have been producing 

» blg results, The building industry, 
for example, has been deliberate- 
ly stimulated. The “continuous pro- 
gress toward recovery. is, in- part 
at least, due to conscious policy 
and the diffusion of 2 more en- 
Ughtened econeme . doctrine, “

DEMOCRAT SPLIT 


Philadelphia, June 22, 

The stage is set for the opening 
of the Democratic Party’s national 
convention, opening to-morrow. 
There may be dramatic develop- 
memts here in the next several 
days ; 

The consensus of impartial opin- 
lon’ is that the re-election as Pre- 
sident of Mr. Frankln D. Roo- 
sevelt, father of the New’ Deal. 
which a few months ago seemed 
assured, ‘can now be secured only 
by strenuous and untiring efforts 
tto round up every available vote.

on President Roosevelt, and his in- 
fluence with the enormous Catholic 
voting power in the United States, 
the allegations of graft in the New

among: the difficulties: facing the 
President ut the outset: of his 
campaign. ;

London, June 22, 
: His’ Majesty the King to-day ap- 
proved tne: promotion of General 
Sir Cyril Deverell to the rank of" 
Field Marshal,.in succession to 


the late Field Marshal Lord Allenby, ~ ™: 


hero, of the Palestine campaign: 
and the man who wok Jerusalem 
Field Marshal Sir Cyril Jonn De~ 
yerell, K.C.B, K.B.E. C.B., has heen " 
A.D.C. General to. the King since. 
1934," and G.0.C.-in-C,  Bastern 
Command since: 1933. He has 
also béen Colonel of the West 
Yorkshire: Regiment (The Prince 
of Wales Own) since 1934, 
He was born in November, ’ ‘1814, 
the son of an army officer, and he 
married the daughter of: the! Co- 
lonel of ‘the Prince of Wales Own 


West Yorkshire Regiment, Colonet ‘ 


1G. Grant-Dalton. 

In 1913-14 he was on the Gener- 
al.Staf in India and with the out-_ 
break of: war was Brigade. Major 
until 1915, when he was placed in. 
command of the 4th Hast Lan- 
cashire Regiment. He won: the 
20th Infantry Brigade, 7th Divi- 
‘sion, that year-and kept the com- 
mand untill 1916 when he was ap- 
Pointed divisional commander, 
with, the famdus Third Division in 
August of that year,’ He °" 
remained Divisional Commander 
untii April, 1919, when’ he went to 
the Northern Division of the Bri- 
tsh Army on the Rhine, 

He’ was promoted Major-General 
for distinguished ‘service: dn. the 
Meld in 1919 and was mentionea in 
despatches nine times, 

After, the War, Field Marshal . 
Deverell continued to serve with 
distinction; being given: post as 
Quarter-Master General in India, 
1927-30, and Chie? of Stat in In- 
dia, 1930- 31, He was made a fun 
general in 1933,— - :

SOUTH-WEST HOPES 
. Canton, June 23. 

Mr, Liu Lu-yin’s speech of ad-

‘the grave task of resisting Japan- 
ese aggression, the South-west au-

pedition.

Jerusalem, June 23. 

Arab guerilla bands, which are 
becom! ig ‘more and more aggres- 
sive day by day, attacked a train 
on Monday ‘which was escorted by 
a detachment of British troops, 
opening heavy fire and! ' raining 
bombs upon’ the train which they 


had: Yorced. to halt by putting | 


large pieces of: rock on the track. 


The British: troops replied with 


machine gun fire. 

‘While the battle was in Progress 
the ‘Arabs placed rocks on the rails 
behind the train which was thus 
virtually. caught in a trap, being 
unable to move either backwards 
or forwards. The British military 
escort finally succeéded in repuis- 
Ing the Arabs who left seven dead 
behind them. Two British soldiers 
are reported to have been wound- 
ed. ‘ : a 

The Arab insurgents again tam- 
‘pered with the rain water_ upon 
which Jerusalem is dependent for 
its water supply so that the elty 
is once more deprived of its water 
supply. ‘

CURFEW ORDERED , 
Jerusalem, June 23. , 

In. order to tighten precautions 
against Arab snipers and ambush, 
curfew has been ordered between 
seven in the evening and 4.30 in 
the morning along the road trom 
Jerusalem to Haifa and no traffic 
will be allowed thereon. Pedes- 
trians are not allowed to approach 
within five hundred yards on both 
sides of the road. 

A. bomb was .thrown in the’ 
Jewish’ quarter of the city, but’ 
there were no casualties, 
another failed to explode.

MECHANICAL PARKING . 


‘yhe United States has a new 
gadget. It is called the parking 
"meter. It is used for regulating 
the. parking. of cars . : 

Slot machines. are erected for, 
every car at esch parking place. « 
As you, park your car you put w! 
nickel (5 cents).into the machine.' 
The fee entitles you to.an hour’s,: 
parking, and o green light shows. | 

When the hour Is up a red lignt | 
‘shdws and anhther fee must then | 
be paid to release the car or to; 
obtain a further period. 

The system was introduced by 
Oklahoma a few months ago, has 
been adopted ‘since: then by Texas, ' 


Flotida and Michigan, and is Hkely - 


to spread to every State.

ARCHBISHOP OF CANTERBURY, 

AND CHINA 

The Archbishop of Canterbury, 
Presiding at the annual reunion of 
the Hong Kong and South Ghina 


Diocesan Association, held at Mer- | 


chant Taylors' Hall, saiq that to 
_ him China was increasingly a rid- 
dle to which. it seemed impossible 
to find a‘tiue. But behind and 
beneath all that seemed to us in- 
‘ explicable restlessness and contu- 
sion there way the inherlrance of 
an ancient civilization und the, 
_ underlying character of the peo- 
ple. Governments came and went, 
generals moved hither and thither, 
but the Chinese people seenled: to 
remain steadfast m their ‘Industry. 
in their patience,” in their good 
yhumour and trustworthidess. He 
was certain that a nation that had 
. its foundation in such a character 
“must have a great future. ‘There 
/ was also an age. -long enthusiasm 
‘for education, dn enthusiasm 
which seemed to transcend differ- 
ences o1 Governments and régimes. 
Everywhere throughout’ China 
there was ceaseless endeavour -to 
provide schools and education. One 
‘could not but think thatin thls 
restlessness, and uncertainty there 
were the signs ofa great people 
awakening to a new. life. If that 
‘were ‘so, then they must all have 
a great desire that Christianity 
=, should have a place, in the mould- 
ing of the Inevitably great future 
which was coming to China.

DENIES AIMING 
AT PRESIDENCY 


More Useful To 
Country In- Present 
Re ie, . Capacity 


q USES OF MOBILITY 


i + 
Nanking, “June 17. 

Generalissimo. Caiang Kal-shek 
suys that he has no intention of 
being a cundidate for President. 
This statement was made to @ Te- 
presentative ‘of the “North China 
Daily News" in the course of an 


interview to-day. When question-. 
ed about the forthcoming assembly 


in November, and the great impor- | 


tance of its agenda, Generalissimo! 
Chiang Kal-shek decidedly express~ 
ed the view that he would not be- 
come a. candidate for Presidént, 
and added that “the purpose of, 
the: November Assembly is to en- 
able rhe people as a.whole to par- 
ticipate In political affairs;.to take 
upon themselves’ the responsibility 
ot saving ‘the country, and unltedly 
to solve national problems.” , 

“The Kuomintang feels,” ‘added 
‘the Generalissimo, “that unless the 
‘whole country is together it is im- 
possible to save China. Therefore, 
the November Assembly has been 
called to promulgate constitution- 
al laws; to return to the people 
Political power, and to invest them 
with the authority to form,a gov- 
ernment to take over the respon- 
sibilities ,of the tutelaty period of 
the Kuomintang.’ This is the un- 
animous opinion of ¢ 
Party, and I am not aware’ of any 
member having any presidential 
ambitions in his mind.’ Every 
member realises that only if we 
can all accept responsibility, be law 
abiding, and willing to sacrifice all 
Personal feelings concerning ‘posi-. 
tion, power, and benefits, can thy 
country be saved.” 

Asked why he /s unwilling to be- 
come a Presidential candidate, the 
Generalissimo explained that “at 
this critical juncture of the coun- 
try’s history everyone should exert 
himself, to the utmost for the bene- 
fit of the nation, and Ido not feel 
that I would be doing “that if I 
sought.’ presidentia} honours, I 
feel that my greatest contribution 
to national needs comes through 
being able at any time to visit near 
and distant parts of the country, to 
observe the requirements of the 
people. and be available at any 
time to assist in the solution of the 
varlous problems connected with 
military reorganization, education:’ 
al reforms, economic reconstruc- 
tion, rural. amelioration, inter- 
provincial communications, and-all 
other questions affecting the 
fundamental welfare of the peo- 
ple,” 

‘PERSONAL CONTACTS 


In reply to a question as to his 
impressions gained during his 
numerous trips to distant parts of 
the country during the past few 
years the Generalissimo said: "“Ex- 
perience has shown that more pro- 
gress has been made during the 
past two or three.years at places I 
have been able to visit than. at any 
previous time because. of the per- 
sonal contacts that were made and 
maintathed with provincial .and 
local officials and people, and ‘that 
ts why I feel that maintenance of 
mobility on my part {is of extreme 
importance. ‘It follows that such 
contacts, “removing .as they have 
done difficulties and misunder- 
' standings, and:simplifying the alms 
of national policy, have brought to. 
the people a growirlg sense of the 
oneness of the nation and inspired 
a quickening of national loyalty to 
the Central Government.” 

“As President,” the ".Generalis- 
simo added, “it would be impossi- 
ble to continue such wide and far-. 


. flung contacts, for the simple rea- 


son that presidential duties require. 
almost constant presence in the 
‘capital. As Ihave sald.” the Gen- 
erallssimo concluded very earnest- 
ly. “I feel” that mobility on my part 
is'my best contribution to the Up-, 
building of China, and because I 
do not wish to have ‘that mobility 
curtailed I have no intention of 
allowing myself to be nominated as 
a candidate for the” presidency.”—

London, June ls. 

This” year’s “Who's “Who” says 
that Mr. J... Thomas was: born 
at New port in ‘1874, writes a cor- 
respondent) . 78 

It adds that he was educated at 
the Council schools, “commenced 
work at nine years of age as an 
errand boy; from that to engine~ 
cleaner, and stage to stage as fire- 
man .and engine-driver (G.W:R.),” 
He became Labour M.P. for Derby, 
“Who's Who" records, in 1910. , y: 

TI Rad’ cause to- day to refer to 
Debrett's “House of Commons and 
the .Judicial Bench” for 1911. 
Therein I came upon a surprising 
entry under the name of J. H. Tho- 
mas, Derby. It said: : 

James ‘Henry Thomas, b 1a! m 
early life emigrated to Australla,. 
and was | Pres. + of, “Amalgamated 
Miners Asso., and 
‘Strike Defence Committee, Broken 
Hill, Australia, 1890; sat in NS. 
Wales Parliament 1894-1901. A 
Labour Member; has sat for Derby | 
since Jan. 15th. Y910. Address, 
House of Commons, Westminster, 
S.W. 


PRODIGY 


“Who's Who” credits Mr. Tho- 

mas with a spectacular career; 
and, incidentally, “Who's Who" of 
1911 gave precisely the same ac- 
count of ‘his early struggles as does 
the 1936 edition, But it Debrett's 
“House of Commons” for’ 1911 1s 
to be believed, “Wno’s Who” gross- 
ly understates the facts. 
- For during those years when, ac- 
cording to “Who’s Who.” Mr. Tno- 
mas won his way from errand boy 
to cleaner, and cleaner to freman 
and driver on the Great Western 
Railway, he wevertheless found 
time, according to Debrett,.to go 
to Australla and achleve a meteoric 
rise in Labour and political spheres 
there. 

And meteoric ts ha word. True, 
Debrett gave us the date of Mr. 
«homas’s birth only to the nearest 
century; but if we accept. the 
statement of “Who's Who” that ne 
was barn in 1874, then he was im- 
parting wise counsel to the Broken 
Hill Strike Defence Committee at 
the age of 18. and tothe New 
South Wales Parliament at 20. 


WHEN WIRELESS ‘WAS NOT 
- UNIVERSAL 


The pausing of Sir Archibald 
Denny recalls to a reader an in- 
cident .whjch illustrates the grip 
that home has on the Scot-in exile. 
On a voyage from the Clyde to. 
Burmah in 1913 .our'contact with‘ 
the world at large and ‘knowledge 
Of passing events depended on the 
news we received at different ports 
of call. At Perim we heard of the 
winning of the Derby and other 
\tems of “national” tmportance. 


THE NEWS AT RANGOON 


Thereafter. we were completely 
out of touch with things. until we 
arrived at Rangoon. stter an ex- 
change of greetings with cnmpat- , 
riots from Dalla ‘Dockyard, who 
had come on board, we were na- 
turally keen to kuow what nua 
heen happening while we were 
on the high seas, so some one ask- 
ed, “What's the news?” Instantly 
came the answer—‘Dumbarton is | 
into the First League and Archie 
Denny has’ been made 2 baronet!”

Shanghai, June 19.

believe that in a few years a motor

quite commonplace. i

in Indo-China. In support of this,

the truth of thelr statement.

PLANE LANDS ON 
SHIP’ s DECK 


Unique Receriaice For 
. Normandie ° 


London, Jiine 22. 

A curious accident occurred in 
Cowes Road. where a Royal Air 
Force seaplane made ~ forced 
landing.on the. deck of the giant 
French liner, Normandie.. 

The Normandie was. tranaferring 
passengers and cargo bi tenders at 
the time. 

The seaplane struck a wing on a, 
derrick which was lifting a motor- 
car, causing the car to hit the 


‘Normandle’s rails and resting half 


aboard and half .autboard of the 
ship,, 

The plane, by a marvellous ‘stroke 
ot tuck, rested upright and ‘vas 
not seriously damaged. It perched 
on the forecastle.deck.! 

The ‘seaplane apparently got into 
an air pocket above the Norman- 
die’s funnels and narrowly avoided 
being sucked down Anto one of 
them. ' 

The liner took the machine to, 
Le Havre :

BELGIAN WORKERS 
TO RESUME 


Brussels; June 22, 


The Socialist, Trade Union Com-' 


mittee which controls the bulk of 
the 400,000 strikers has ordered a 
resumption of work on Wednesday, 
the objects of the strike having 
been attained for an immense 
majority of the workers. 

Workers ‘still resisting their em-~- 


" ployets are assured of the full sup- 


port of the Trade Union organtsa- 
tlon.— :

PLANES CATAPULTED AT. 
90 MPH. 


Germany's third floating aerodrome, 


London, June 10. 
the Ostmark, ‘recent y 


built at the Howaits, Works, Kiel for the Lufthamsa, sailed from 


German waters few days‘ago, 


and ly expected to take, Bp her ‘ 


position off Bathurst, British Gambia, carly next week. 


Whereas: the Westfajen and the 
Schwabenland, the two other. ves- 
sels now in use in connection with 
the South American air-mall ser- 
vice are ordinary steamers recon- 
‘structed to enable aeroplanes to be’ 
catapulted. from, their decks. the 
Ostmark has: ‘been specially built 
as. a “floating aerodrome.” 

As Hitherto, two vessels—on2 
stationed off the African coast. 


the, other off the coast of ‘Brazil— ‘ 


will serve as “floating islands," 
and the purpose’of putting the | 
third ship into service is to enable 
them to be released in turn from 
time to time for overhauling, 


“PRESTIGE OF THE’ REICH” 


~The’ Ostmark, it is “explained. 
has been. so named because like 
that East Prussian frontier dis- 
trict “it will work for the prestige 
oc"the Reich at an advansed post 
\and give. expression at the same 


time to the very close association |" 


existing between the most modern 
method of transport and the, East 


e of Germany.” 


With a displacement of 2,000 
tons’ a length of about’ 350 feet 
and a beam of about 40 feet, the 
Ostmark ls. substantially smaller 


“Ithan Westfalen or the Schwaben- 


land. . Her two .Diesel engines 
each develop 950 h.p., and:she can 
teach a speed of about fourteen 
knots. : 

‘Like the Westfalen and Schiva- 
benland the Ostmark is equipped 
with & ‘catapult ‘arrangement, by 
means of . wolch a 1%ton Dornier 
flying-boat—-the .type use on the 
South American mail- service—can 
be projected into the alr at a 
speed of some ninety miles an 
hour; but technically she.is a 
great improvement on her sister 
ship. 

Unlike in the case of the Schwa- 


benland, the fiying-boats will be; 


projected from the deck of the 
Ostmark- over. the bows. 

The arrangement of the rails 
from which the flying-boat is ac- 
tually catapulted is noteworthy in 
that they .take, up the middle of 
tite deck lengthwise. 

.The rear part of the projector- 
rails connects «with . turn-table, 
thus enabling a second fiying-boat 
to be taken on board. 

“It “zequired, both flying-boats 
can be shot off in succession. 

An electrically-driven crane of a 
Special construction) ‘Is used to 
hotst the fiying-boat on the “float- 
ing aerodrome” aver the stern. 
The post of the crane can be lald 


fiat on the rails, and the conning, 


bridge is: divided on this account. 

The upright member| of the 
crane is shaped Iike a mast in or- 
der to serve as an aerial and !ike- 
wise provide a istand for the 
searchlight. 


LANDING APRON 


\To facilitate the 1 fting on 
board of 2 fiying-boat at,sea the 
Ostmark 1s equipped with a land- 
ing: apron. As the motor-poat,} 
come down, this landing apron is 
flooded in such a fash-on'as to 
cause the motor-ship—the “fioat- 
ing .asrodrome’—to reduce speed; 
and after the landing has been 
effected: in the apron ‘the vessel 
puts on speed. , 

This increase of speed .of the 
boat then -eauses the landing 
apron to stiffen, and the flying- 
boat becomes. raised somewhat out 
of the water; by this proceeding 
the motor-stip. and flying-boat 
are connected more firmly together, 
therdby enabling the aeroplane to 
be..taken on board: without ald 
even in a Ghoppy sea, °' 

To meet the great’ requirements 
of safety in the dlr’ service over 
the South Atlantic the “float'ng 
aerodrome” has a wireless station 
of the..most up-to-date kind and 


fitted cut with .a direction-finder 
‘| aerial, and an. emergenty trans- 


mitter. 


Because (as ‘already mentioned): 


the” projector-ralis. stretch. down 


almost the ‘whole length of the 


vessel a special arrangement for 
‘fxing the aerial has pee eons: 


Thos ‘a-mast has ‘been: set an on 
each side of the ‘projéc ir-track.. 


: and trom these masts: the ‘anten 


; Mme Gre carried over to the rear 
crang-mast. In the catapulting oi 
& flying-boat these masts can be 

clapped down outboard so that 
they no longer are an obstacle in 
the way of the flying-boat when it 
Starts. 

In view of the constant employ 
ee of the’ boat in the tropics, 
the needs of the crew are specially 

‘ catered for. Their accommoda- 

. tion is Toomy and equipped in the 

| most modern fashion, ‘There {3 a 
wireless set with loud-speaker, a 

| cinema, a good | arary, and varl- 


ous games, 


Since the Lufthansa started 
their regular’ air service from Ger- 
many over the Atlantic to South 
America on February 3- 1934, about 
180 scheduled serial filgits have 
been. carrled out without mishap.

FIRST CREMATORIUM 
FOR SHANGHAI

City Government " 


Shanghai, June 18. 
Octuping 20 mow of land, tne 


| frst crematorium, will’ be buut by |. 


the ShanghaL city Government in

tional Quarantisie Service, will be 
responsibley for the. collection ot 
$10,000 - contributions, while , Mr. 
Yih Yu-Hu, Mr. Kwan Chun-tse, 
Mr. Wang‘ Yeh-tse and Mr, Wang 
Ih-ting, for the remaining $10,000. 


The crematorium is planned to 
relieve tMe congestion.of cheap 
coffins temporarily. Jald'on fields in 
different parts of the city. The 
apthorities hold that these coffins 
will not” only prove detriment- 
al to public’ health but a waste. of 
good ground. Under the duapices 
of the” City Government, 4a pre- 
Paratory committee was organized

tse, Major-Gen, Tsai Ching-chun.

Chun-tse, Dr. Shen Yih, Dr. Li 
Ping an, Mr. Wang Chang-chun 
and Mr. Wu Tsin-ya.

ernment.—(N.C, DN),

‘CAREER 


ITS ADVANTAGES . 
(Special Air Mafl Service) 
London, June 15. 
| In the last 100 years the Navy 
has never lacked the numbers, 
either of -officers pr men, which it 
has needed: for deep in the con-' 
sciousness of the, nation {is the 
Tealizatton of the truth of Mr. 
Ramsay MacDonald’s. words Ir. 


. opening the London Naval Con- 


ference of 1930—“Ihe way cf 
Great Britain {son the sea; our 
Navy {s no mere superfiulty to us; 
{t ts us.’ There is hardly a village 
in the country but can boast of 
at least one of its sons serving in 
the Fleet. elther on the iower deck 
or the quarter deck; and there is 
no finer career for the Englishman. 
Yet of Inte years the Navy has 
been somewhat in eclipse with 
those in ‘search of a career for 
thelr sons, and the reason Is not 
far to seek. At the end of ‘the 
War the Navy was left with a 
large surplus of officers and’ 2 
much diminished Fleet. For many. 
years after the War the further. 
diminution of the Fleet was con~ 
tinuous, with the result that the 
measures taken,” by” special 
‘schemes of bonus on retirement 
‘amd the like, to bring the num- 
bers in the lower ranks down to 
the figure, atwhich the “normal 
proportion of them could loo! for 
promotion to the ranks of captain 
and admiral were constantly ren- 
dered out of date. The stagnation 
in promotion persisted, and the 
consequence way that many first- 
class officers who deserved “and 
were fully qualified for promotion 


‘} crowd: 


were superannuated for’ lack of 
vacancies, Their fate was a dis- 
couragement to their sons and the 
sons of their friends. © . ‘ 
| THE EXAMPLE . . 
That phase has now passed. 
The exampie in unilateral dis-~ 
armament seb: by this cotintry has: 
not been follbwed abroad, and the 
present urgent necessity. tor mak- 
Ing good deficiencies’ in the~ De- 
fence Services: ensurez’ that for 
Tmany years from now the Navy 
‘will. be an. expanding... Service.
